--- Setting a Budget ---

1
Now, one thing
2
that people worry about, justifiably,
3
is cost overruns while learning
4
or playing around in the cloud.
5

6

7

8

9
you went off to go for dinner, you go to sleep,
10

11

=== Azure Virtual Machines ===


--- Overview of Azure VM and App Services ---

1
So let's talk about some of the services
2
that overall can be considered Azure compute.
3
The first service that we'll talk about
4
is the virtual machine.
5
Now, virtual machines
6
are Azure's Infrastructure as a Service offering,
7
or IaaS for short.
8
You can deploy either Windows or Linux virtual machines,
9
you can connect to them via RDP or SSH,
10
and you'll be able to treat these machines
11
just as if they're a physical server.
12
But it actually isn't a complete server,
13
it's a virtualized server,
14
or a server that has been split up into segments
15
that individual customers like ourselves can use.
16
Now, just like in real-life,
17
virtual machines must be placed on a network.
18
In the case of Azure, it's called a virtual network,
19
and you have the ability to arrange them in various ways,
20
depending on your needs.
21
So if you put them in an availability set,
22
you can arrange so that they're not gonna be all taken down
23
for maintenance at the same time.
24
If you were to put them into availability zones,
25
you're protecting against building-level outages,
26
such as power outages and internet outages,
27
you can place them behind load balancers
28
to provide maximum availability to your customers,
29
et cetera.
30
Since they are the operating systems
31
of either Windows or Linux,
32
you have administrative connectivity to them,
33
you can install whatever software you wish,
34
just as if you had a server in the back room.
35
So you have the full control to install whatever you need.
36
And it usually can only take a few minutes
37
from the time that you start the process
38
of starting a virtual machine or creating a virtual machine
39
until it is provisioned and it's ready to go for you.
40
So, within 10 minutes, you can have a VM of your own.
41
Now there are other services in Azure
42
that are made up of virtual machines behind the scenes.
43
And so, for instance, the Azure Batch service
44
lets you run large-scale parallel jobs.
45
So let's say you are a drug-maker
46
and you need to run a large-scale test
47
of a particular chemical reaction, you need to model that,
48
you can have hundreds of virtual machines
49
running the same task and reporting their results
50
back to some central location,
51
that would be a typical batch job.
52
We have the concept of scale sets, or VMSS for short,
53
which lets you automatically scale VMs in and out,
54
so if you have two or four or eight
55
or 16 virtual machines running,
56
they all serve the clients independently
57
and you can add to them if you need more capacity.
58
We're gonna talk about the details of each of this
59
as we get into those sections of the course.
60
You have containers like Azure Kubernetes Service, or AKS,
61
that is a fully-managed Kubernetes environment,
62
those run on virtual machines, but they run as nodes,
63
and there's an orchestrator that manages them.
64
And, finally, you have a service called Service Fabric,
65
which is a platform for building microservices,
66
although, it is used less often these days.
67
Now that we live in a containerized world,
68
containers are way more popular for microservices
69
than Service Fabric.
70
Not all of these are going to be on the exam,
71
so I'll point that out.
72
Next up, we'll talk about app services.
73
Now, Azure App Services is fully-managed platform
74
for running web apps or containers
75
without managing the underlying server.
76
So it's really an abstraction on top of a virtual machine.
77
You can also choose Windows or Linux operating system
78
for app services,
79
although with that abstraction,
80
the difference matters a little bit less.
81
It supports a wide range of frameworks,
82
such as .NET, Java, Node.js, and others.
83
App services also add a number of built-in benefits,
84
like auto-scaling, deployment slots,
85
continuous integration, continuous deployment,
86
and even compatibility with Visual Studio.
87
These are going to be on the exam,
88
these two topics, of course.
89
So that's it for the compute services
90
we'll cover at this high-level,
91
I guess that we have individual sections and videos
92
covering them later in the course.
93
Next up, we're gonna talk about storage.


=== Automate deployment of resources by using templates ===


--- Overview of Azure Storage and Data Services ---

1
Next up, we'll talk about storage.
2
Now, the first type of storage
3
is sometimes called unmanaged storage,
4
or just a storage account in Azure.
5
One of the great features of this is
6
you can have almost unlimited amount of data
7
in these things.
8
It's scalable into the petabytes range,
9
and, if you fill that up,
10
you can certainly create more storage accounts.
11
So it's really an expandable storage option
12
even for on-premises applications.
13
Now, within a general purpose storage account,
14
there are four types of data that you can store
15
all within the same storage account.
16
They are blobs, queues, tables, and file share.
17
We are gonna get into blobs and file shares in this course.
18
Queues and tables are not covered by this exam.
19
Now, storage accounts are highly scalable
20
with built-in redundancy options,
21
so you can have local storage redundancy
22
or even global storage redundancy.
23
And, in terms of access,
24
you can have either hot, or cool, or cold,
25
or even archive tiers,
26
which allows you to pay different amounts,
27
either a little bit more expensive, a little bit cheaper,
28
based on how frequently data is accessed.
29
And there is such a thing as a managed storage account,
30

Instructor: Next up, we'll talk
about what are called microservices,

of multiple small applications
all working together to perform a job.
And so instead of having one big
monolithic program with millions
of lines of code,

that can be operated independently,

=== Configure name resolution ===


--- What is Microsoft Entra ID (used to be Azure Active Directory)? ---

Instructor: One of the first concepts
we're going to have to deal with in this course
is the concept of identity and identity management.
Now, Microsoft recently made a huge switch
in the branding of their identity products,
which used to be called Azure Active Directory
and has now been renamed to Entra ID.
So basically, you're going to find a mixture
in the internet in general,
perhaps even in this course where I mention Azure AD.
And what you should be thinking about
is it's now called Entra ID.
Even I'm still getting used to
thinking about this product as Entra,
because I've been calling it Azure AD
for seven plus years now.
So the concept of identity
within Azure and identity management
is sort of one of the central tenets of security.
Good security policy means having good identity management.
The identity is what you're going to hand out to your users,
their ID and password,
or maybe you're gonna create a multifactor authentication
so there's additional elements beyond password,
but basically you're gonna need a system
to manage the back end of people's identities
and to secure that.
And so in Microsoft Azure,
the native platform is called Entra.
Now, Entra is derivative
of one of the most successful identity management products
in the world of Microsoft Active Directory.
So Active Directory, not to be confused now with Entra,
is the on-premises identity management.
It is handling networks, objects,
users, groups on-premises.
There's no cloud involved in a Microsoft Active Directory.
And so what Microsoft did was they extended that concept
into the cloud with Azure Active Directory,
and now with Entra.
And so Entra is a cloud native version
of handling users, groups and identity in the cloud.
Now, there is a lot of similarities
between Microsoft Active Directory and now Entra.
Think of it as having a lot of overlap,
but they're not replaceable, interchangeable products.
You cannot just get rid of your company's on-premises AD
and turn everything into Entra accounts
without a lot of effort in terms of doing that,
making your whole business
into a hybrid environment, et cetera.
All right,
and they don't have overlapping features and functions.
There's some overlapping features and functions,
but there's some things that on-premises AD does
that cloud AD doesn't.
So for instance, there's some protocols
that work not over the internet,
so they're not compatible with HTTP or TCP.
That would be Kerberos or LDAP and things like that,
where those are not compatible features
over the broad internet.
And so that's why we had to use internet compatible methods.
And so that is where you get protocols like OAuth and SAML.
And so in this section and the videos that follow,
we're gonna get into talking about Entra,
how you are provided one
when you set up a brand new account,
what you can do with it
in terms of managing users and groups,
how applications, custom applications
that you develop can use Entra
to manage the identity of their users
and do authentication through that,
as well as third party products
such as Dropbox and OneDrive
that you can validate through Entra
to give people access to certain files and folders
in third party products.
So Entra is a huge topic and there's a lot of moving parts.
It's gonna be very difficult for me
to give you a full overview of Entra in a single video.
And so in this video
and the video that follows,
we're gonna be talking about all the different ways
that we can do identity and access management
in the cloud using Entra.

=== Configure load balancing ===


--- Managing Multiple Directories ---

-: So in this video we're gonna be talking
about managing more than one tenant.
So we have these concepts of account, tenant,
and subscription, and they are three independent things.
The account or the user is either a person or a program.
So it is the email address and the password
that you use to log into the Azure portal
that makes you a user or an account.
Sometimes you have MFA enabled
and that's part of the authentication process as well.
Applications can also have a user concept,
and that's called a managed identity,
where they use identity managed by active directory
to do their work under the permissions
that have been granted to that identity.
It's probably bad practice for an application to
use a real person's credentials 'cause as soon
as you change the password, it breaks the application.
As soon as the person quits their job or is let go,
then the application's gonna stop working when
the account is disabled.
So make unique users for your applications,
called managed identity.
A completely separate concept is the tenant.
And so the tenant is the organization,
it's the business, it's the corporation.
Tenants have domain names usually,
either you have one assigned to you
or you can assign an existing one to Azure Active Directory.
You cannot assign the same domain name to
multiple active directory tenants.
And so if you're gonna choose example.com,
no other Azure ID account can have that as the domain name.
Basically Azure ID is managing the domain that
you provide it.
And so as we're saying,
you basically have to have a tenant in order to do anything,
in order to log into Azure, do any kind of work,
you do require a tenant.
Now the third concept is a subscription.
That is the billing arrangement.
You can have a free subscription, pay as you go,
enterprise agreement,
you work with a vendor like a CSP type of arrangement,
many different types of subscriptions, that is independent
to your tenant, independent to your user.
Now subscriptions can of course be assigned to tenants.
Not every tenant has one.
So I can create a tenant that doesn't have a subscription
and therefore can't create any resources inside of it,
or tenants can even have more than one subscription.
So anytime you create a resource
you are asked to choose the subscription.
That's because tenants can have more than one subscription.
And it's not restricted to having a single account.
So you can assign any number of accounts
to your tenant and they have different roles, everything
from being an owner, contributor, reader, and hundreds
of smaller subdivisions of those types of roles.
So that is the concept of subscription, tenant, and user.

--- Manage Azure AD / Microsoft Entra ID Groups ---

Lecturer: All right, so the purpose of this next lesson,

to operate on users in bulk.
One of the ways that is offered is what's called a group.
So in Azure AD a group is a collection of users
who have been given specific access permissions
to resources in the Azure cloud platform.
So in my example that I'm just going through,
I created three students and three teachers,

--- Administrative Units ---

Instructor: So we really haven't talked about
the concept of roles yet
and role-based access control.
There's a whole separate section following this section
talking about roles and permissions.
But in this video we're gonna move on to talk about
what's called administrative units.
Now this is an interesting concept.
What I'm gonna do before we continue is I'm going to
assign a role to the teachers that gives them some kind
of permissions within our Azure active directory.
So I'm gonna look at the help desk role.
Azure is pretty slow today.
So there's a help desk role
and I'm going to assign the three teachers,
1, 2 and 3 into the help desk role.
Now, all you need to know right now
is that this gives them certain permissions
to be able to let's say, reset the password of the students.
Okay? Now, the way that I've set this up right now
is each teacher can be a help desk user
for all of the other users in AD.
There's no differentiation
between which teacher can help which students right now.
So it's almost like a role for your whole company
is that these people all have equal access
to all users within your Azure AD, okay?
Now, the concept of an administrative unit
is a way of segregating these permissions
into some type of logical separation.
So you can delegate administrative permissions
and control over specific areas of the directory
to specific administrators.
In practicality, what this allows you to do is
have certain administrative users, in this case
the help desk users, to only be able to affect certain part
of your organization, not the entire organization.
So this is really a segregation of administrators
into pairs where they do their work.
Now this makes logical sense
if you have your whole company,
let's say you've got 10,000 users
in this Azure active directory
but they're broken into different departments.
And so you have your customer service department,
you have your marketing department,
you have your sales team on the front lines,
and you may have different help desk users
helping each of them.
They're on the floor with them,
they work side by side together with them
but you don't necessarily want your help,
your customer service help desk to be touching
the sales team's accounts.
So this is a way of segregating it.
So let's create an administrative unit.
I'm gonna call this the...
Let's call this classroom 1
'cause we're dealing with students and teachers.
So this is the classroom 1 administrative unit
and which roles are we going to grant access
to AD for this?
So let's see this password administrator role
and we're gonna say teacher 1.
So we've got classroom 1
and we've got teacher 1 who's a password administrator.
Now, classroom 1 exists
and teacher is the password administrator for classroom 1
but classroom 1 doesn't have any members.
So I'm gonna put student 1 in classroom 1.
And so now teacher 1 can change the password
for student 1.
You see what I mean?
So this administrative unit allows you
to segregate which administrative users can work
with which parts of your organization.
And of course it doesn't have to be only users
that you're dealing with.
Maybe your teacher can work with groups.
We talked about groups earlier, and so maybe
teacher 1 is the administrator for the student group
but they can't touch any other teacher account.
So hopefully you can see that administrative units
allows you to segregate your active directory such that
certain administrators only have permissions
to work on certain areas.

--- Manage Devices ---

Host: So the next concept within Azure AD
that we'll talk about is the concept of a device.
Now, this might seem strange,
we're talking about users in groups and roles,
but what's a device?
Well, devices represent and manage the physical devices
used by users that access organization's resources.
So if you're logging into your applications
from your work computer inside the office,
well that would be represented in here as a device.
If you're working from home
or working from your phone or from another device,
as long as you're authenticating through Azure
that will be listed as a device.
Now, in my example here
I don't have any devices that are managed.
They're all unmanaged devices.
So what does it mean that a device is managed?
Well, a managed device
you could have certain minimum standards
in terms of encryption, password requirements,
that you have to have an antivirus running
or malware protection.
You can set certain organizational standards
and say that only devices that meet these standards
can access this application.
The devices are also used
in some of the other security features,
such as conditional access.
So we haven't talked about conditional access yet,
but conditional access allows you to restrict access
to your applications based on a number of factors.
It could be the geographical location of the user,
it could be that they're using a pre-approved device.
So let's say the person is logging into your network
using their company laptop
and you know that it's their company laptop
and it follows all of the minimum standards
for a versioning, security, encryption, all those things.
Well then you can not require multifactor authentication
if that person is using that registered device.
But if they're using their home laptop
and it doesn't meet those standards, then you can ensure
that you can enable multifactor authentication.
So conditional access allows you to have certain rules
based on certain features
and so the devices that people use
can be sets of those features.
So here's an example of, again,
these are the devices that are associated with this tenant
and they're all, happen to be Windows devices
and we can see various information about the OS,
the name of the device,
what OS version that they're running,
which user is using the device, when it was registered,
and when the last activity was observed.
Right now, these are all unmanaged devices.
If I wanted to,
I could select my computer,
and well, for instance, in this particular case,
I can disable the device altogether.
Now we can see a limited set of settings here,
the main one under the device settings.
So right off the bat, we can basically allow users
or deny them the ability to even join their devices.
We can require multifactor authentication
in order for the device to be registered or joined
and maximum number of devices per user here.
Okay, we can even manage BitLocker keys within Azure AD.

--- Overview of RBAC ---

Scott: So in this section of the course
we're gonna be talking about Role Based Access Control.
This term is abbreviated as RBAC or "R-back".
So RBAC is a security feature in Azure
that allows you to grant specific permissions
to users, groups, or applications
based on their roles in your organization.
So you get very granular control over permissions,
that allows you to have increased security
as well as increased compliance
with your various regulations and policies.
Now, at a very basic level,
in order for a user to be able to do something,
either within your application
or within Microsoft Azure itself,
it needs to have permission,
and what this means is that there's a table somewhere
within Azure of all of the users
and all of the permissions that those users have
to act within your environment.
At a very basic level,
we can look at something like this
and say we want to grant Student 1, which is a user,
read access to this storage account, Storage account 1,
and that is a single permission listed in a table.
But if you think about a larger organization
with tens of thousands of users
and perhaps thousands of resources
including storage accounts, and virtual machines,
and apps, and not only read access,
but write access and ownership access,
all of these types of permissions
become very difficult to manage.
You can take your 10,000 users
multiplied by 1,000 odd permissions that they can have,
and that gives you millions of permissions
that would be stored in this type of table.
So what is the drawback to this,
assigning individual permissions to individual users?
Well, Microsoft has something
called the Principle of Least Privilege.
It's more of a general policy within IT
and that is, in general, to have the best chance
of having a high security system,
you want your users to have the fewest amount
of permissions that they can have.
So don't just grant everybody
in your organization administrative permissions,
try to be more granular and grant read permissions
to people who only need to read stuff,
update permissions to people
who do need to interact with the data,
but don't need to own the data,
and be very selective
of who you give administrative permissions to.
So if you're doing this types
of one-on-one permissions-to-users mapping,
you may have some users in your organization
that end up with too many permissions.
You can imagine somebody who's been
with your company for years,
let's say 10 or more years,
have had a lot of roles in your organization,
moving from job to job,
they would then accumulate lots and lots
of IT permissions on their user account,
and their account is one of the more powerful accounts.
Maybe other employees come to that person
when they need assistance
because they have permissions to do things
that the employees don't.
Inversely, you have some employees that are trying
to do things that they should be allowed to do
that end up not having permissions.
You can have a brand new employee and it might take months
before you assign them all of the right permissions
that they need to do their job.
So, this increases the cost and the effort
in order to maintain your permission system.
You are gonna have a help desk
that's gonna receive inquiries every day
asking for permissions to do things,
then they have to wait for those tickets to be responded.
It slows everything down and you end up
with lower productivity across the organization,
and because you do have users
that have too many permissions,
you have a higher chance of some type of attack,
identity attack, where a malicious user gains access
to your system and can do things
that you don't expect them to be able to do.
So there is a system called Role Based Access Control.
In this case, there's this concept of a role,
which is like an abstraction.
When you create a role,
you're basically defining a job
that someone can potentially need to do,
may not map to your job titles,
but you can sort of generalize what the roles are
in your organization.
Who needs read only-access, who needs to be contributor,
and who needs ownership access to various resources.
You can loosely define those
into an abstraction called roles,
and then you assign those roles to individuals.
So, examples of roles could be a developer role,
a manager, development manager role,
or an IT operations role,
maybe just somebody who needs to view reports,
and doesn't want to be able to affect the data in any way,
and there's obviously gonna be
administrators of various sorts,
including global administrators.
These are roles that you can assign individuals to
in your organization.
Now, it doesn't mean that somebody can't be two roles,
so you can be operations and you can also be a developer,
and in this particular example,
you can assign two different roles to the same person,
there would not be any conflict in that.
And so you're just managing permissions at the role level.
You're not assigning permissions to individual users,
you only assign permissions to a role,
and you assign the role to the users,
and, believe it or not,
that actually simplifies the length of the table
in this example for how many permissions you have.
Instead of having millions of permissions
when you have tens of thousands of users
and thousands of resources,
you may only end up with several thousand permissions.
Makes it a lot easier and simpler for everybody.
What are the benefits of Role Based Access Control?
Well, it is a level of abstraction
that does simplify the management of permissions
across your organization.
A simpler management means fewer errors.
As long as you can confirm
that your users are assigned the right roles,
then they should be getting the right permissions.
If you ever get right into a situation
where a particular user needs a permission
that's something outside of their role,
then maybe you either need to define a new role
or they need to be also a member,
additionally a member of a second role.
That's the decision you're making,
not "Should I assign a new permission to a one-off user."
Basically, all users are treated the same for their role.
All developers have the same permissions.
Nobody has secret extra permissions.
When I say secret, it just means you do it once,
and then by tomorrow you've forgotten
that you've assigned this permission to somebody,
and then months and years later,
then that comes back and it bites you.
And it's also easier
for brand new employees who get started,
to just be assigned the developer role
and not have to go back
and find the 250 things that a developer can do.
Now, that's not the only approach to security.
There's also Claims Based Access Control or CBAC,
but in the Microsoft Azure world,
we tend to gravitate towards Role Based Access Control.
So it's one approach to simplifying permissions
and simplifying those assignments.
So in this video, and then in the videos that follow,
we're going to look at Microsoft Azure portal,
and how to create roles and assign roles to individuals.

--- Resource Group Scope ---

Instructor: Now we've previously discussed
this concept of scope when it comes to access control.
When you are making a role assignment, you are choosing
the individual, the role, and the scope, okay?

in a real environment, is the resource group scope.
So, it's not uncommon to restrict access to individuals
to certain resource groups.
So you might have a resource group for development resources
and that might have a wide range of people
who have permissions to access those development resources.
And likewise, you might have production resources
in a different resource group
and then you are able to restrict access
to those resources to only specific individuals.
So obviously you want the production individuals
to be only a handful, or as the development individuals
could be dozens or hundreds.
So, that's a resource group scope.

--- Accounts, Subscriptions, and Resource Groups ---

-: So in this section of the course, we're gonna be talking
about subscriptions and resource groups.
And I wanna define some terms
before we get started, this way,
when I'm talking about Azure accounts or Azure subscriptions
you know that they're slightly different concepts.
So the four terms we're gonna talk
about in this video are the Azure account
a tenant, Azure subscription, and a resource group.
So let's start off talking about one
of the more basic concept, which is an Azure account.
Now, in the context of this, it's basically a user id
it's a person, or it also can represent a program.
So you can basically assign an account to an application
so that the application can run its own security context.
It is considered bad programming practice
to take a real person's user ID
and password and program that into an application.
So when we're talking about people
who have access to resources within Azure,
or assigned resources elsewhere in your solution,
you're talking about an account and
that can either be a person who's yourself or someone who
works in your organization, or it could be an application.
And so this is the basis of authentication.
Now we have a section of this course talking
about Azure Active Directory and authentication separately.
But basically this is
this is the top level definition of an account.
Now a related topic is a tenant.
So whereas the account represents the person
the tenant represents the organization or the company
and usually you have a domain name associated with that.
So we live in this modern era where every big company has
its own domain name
and you may want to start using that domain name
for your user IDs in terms of Microsoft Azure.
And so it's gonna be your company .com
as the tenant's domain name.
Now, if you don't have one, Microsoft will sign you one.
So they have this dot on microsoft.com extension.
And so when you create your tenant
it's gonna create the tenant name dot on microsoft.com
and that will be the domain you're given
if you don't provide one.
Now this basically represents what is called a
dedicated instance of Azure Active Directory.
And as I said, we do have a whole section
of this course talking about Azure Active Directory.
So I won't go too far into it here.
It's important to know though, you have this organization
which is a tenant, and every account
belongs to at least one organization.
There's no concept of having an Azure account
and you're not part of any tenant, that's just
you're not an Azure user if that's you.
So when you log in, you're gonna have a default tenant
and that's the tenant that you have.
You can also switch tenants and it's also fairly
easy to create tenants.
And we'll get into that in this course.
Next step, we'll talk about the concept of subscription.
So now we know that the account is the user
and the tenant is the organization.
The subscription is this billing agreement with Microsoft.
And so there's many different types of subscriptions.
You can have a free subscription or pay as you go.
There's enterprise agreements.
There are different programs.
I think there's probably, you know
a dozen different programs that Microsoft has
that are payment plans for subscriptions.
And so the subscription is the level
at which payment gets made.
So you're gonna have a credit card or an enterprise
agreement that the bill is gonna get sent out to.
And so any usage of resources
within Azure gets billed to the subscription.
So we should say that not every tenant has a subscription.
So if you go and create a brand new tenant today
by default, you have no subscription.
Now what this means is the tenant can't create
Azure resources, so there's no billing unit
and therefore there's no way
for you to even create a resource, even a free one.
So you can have tenants without subscriptions
and it's also possible to have multiple subscriptions.
So you might have an organization that has production
and development as two separate billing entities
and you can create two subscriptions for that.
So there is a multi subscription option.
Now accounts are basically assigned roles within the tenant
and so they can be an owner or a contributor or a reader
or you can give very granular role assignments to users.
So when you create a user, you assign them a role
you can actually create owners
and these people have full control of your tenant.
And so you're gonna be careful with that obviously
but it is possible to have more than one owner on a tenant.
And again, you if any account
that you wish you can invite other accounts
to your tenant and then grant them roles as well.
Now I'm gonna talk about resource groups, but
before we do, let's talk about the resources themselves.
So when I see resources in this course
I'm referring to any entity managed by Azure.
And so these are the typical ones, the virtual machines
web apps, the storage accounts, you know, SQL databases
anything that's sort of, you go in there, you create it
it's a resource, but sometimes
these resources themselves create sub resources.
So public IP addresses created when you create a virtual
machine, network interface card, network security group,
there's all sorts of resources
that get created when you create a virtual machine.
And any other of these big resources sometimes
have their own sub resources.
So those are also included as resources.
And just like you can assign accounts,
permissions, and roles within the tenant level
you can also assign people's individual resources.
Finally the resource group is that collection.
It's a way of organizing resources in a subscription.
So I look at it like a folder structure.
You go into your account, you have your subscription.
Under the subscription, you have resource groups.
And so all resources must belong
to one and only one resource group.
Now it makes it fairly easy to do some particular tasks.
You can grant permissions at the resource group level
and so all of the, you know
sort of account can only access the one resource group,
it sandboxes them in.
You can also delete a resource group
and that will delete all the resources inside.
It's one of the quickest ways to clean up what you did.
So it's a way of separating up projects,
keeping unrelated things separate.
You don't want to have a single resource group
with hundreds of resources inside.
And so keeping them separate allows you
to clean up pretty easily.
So those are the four basic concepts
as we get into this section.

--- Resource Locks - ReadOnly and Delete ---

Instructor: So when you have an Azure subscription,
you have the ability to create a number of resources.
One of the risks of this then is you go into a resource,
and then you accidentally delete the wrong resource.
So let's say you're looking at this function,
and you say delete, and then you just,
it says, type the function name, you confirm.
Once you've deleted it, you've deleted it.
And so, there is some requirement for you to have
a type of warning or restriction
on the deletion of resources.
These are called resource locks.
And there are different scopes for these locks.
We'll start with the biggest scope,
which is a subscription level lock.
So if I go into a subscription, let's choose one,
and I search for lock and it says resource locks,
I can create a lock that will prevent any resource
that is part of this subscription
from being either deleted or modified.
So I'm gonna go into resource locks, I'm gonna say add,
give it a name, do not delete.
And I'm gonna choose the delete lock.
And I can even leave a little note saying,
talk to Scott before deleting please.
And if I add this lock,
then all resources that are inside of this scope,
which is the subscription,
will give you a warning message.
And saying you're not able to delete,
unless you then delete the lock.
So the lock needs to be removed
before you can delete a resource.
One advantage of this is you could
not give people permissions to delete locks.
So if you keep the person who can create and delete locks
to be a single person or a small group of people,
then your wide range of users are not gonna be able to go
and accidentally delete resources.
Another type of lock is the read-only lock.
So, read-only.
This prevents deletions and modifications.
So now, you cannot change the settings on a resource.
So for the delete lock, it only stops deletions.
But you can still go in and you go into your app service
and change some of the settings,
and manually scale the instance and things.
For the read-only lock, you are prevented
from making any changes to any resource.
I'm gonna keep these two locks.
So if I go back to the dashboard
and I go to, let's say, my function app.
And then I pick one of these resources
that's on the function app,
like the app itself, let's say.
And then I try to delete, this should fail
because I have a delete lock on the whole subscription.
And you can see, it says failed
because the following scopes are locked,
and it tells me the subscription.
So I do not even get the chance to override it at this time.
I have to go back to the lock and remove the lock
in order to be able to do it.
So I'm gonna go back to the subscription,
go back to the lock,
and I'm gonna remove both of these locks.
Now, like I said, there's multiple scopes here.
So I can also go to the resource group scope,
type lock,
and I can create another delete lock
on the entire resource group for function app.
This way, I can still not delete
any of the resources in this resource group.
So if I do try to delete the function app,
this failure is on the resource group scope.
So you'll see, it's the same message,
but it's a different scope for why it failed.
And you can even go into the individual resource.
So if we go into the function app and we type lock,
we can see that we could create a read-only lock
on this specific resource.
So other resources in the group
are prevented from being deleted.
This resource is prevented from being modified,
so they're cumulative.
This also means you can't allow us,
you can't overwrite
this lock in a subsequent resource
if you've got a lock on the higher level,
which is like the resource group in this case.
You'd have to go up to that resource in order to modify it.
And again, you may not even have permission for that.
Actually, we can see right off of this screen,
if I delete that lock,
I can go to the resource group scope right from here.
And I can go to the subscription scope right from here.
That's a clever addition. Way to go, Microsoft.
So resource locks operate at subscription,
resource group, and individual resource levels.
And allow you to prevent either just deletions,
or modifications and deletions on resources.
And you have the ability to control who has access
to delete or create locks.

If I was to choose a different region, such as UK,

1.9 cents per gigabyte.

Second thing is you want your data
to live close as possible to the places

So if you have your apps
and your virtual machines in one region,
and they access that data in a storage account,
you may want your storage accounts
to be in the same region.
Third, you may have various data residency requirements
that you have to live by.
So again, different countries
and different regions are gonna have laws
where data must be stored for their citizens.
And so if you are, for instance, a European citizen
and you're storing the private data of European citizens,
then perhaps you may have to store that data
in the region of Europe.
So you're gonna choose region
based on a number of different factors.
Oh, finally, there might be various features
that are only available to certain regions.
Microsoft does have the tendency
to roll out new types of things
to one region before another
so that they can be tested out.
So if you are dependent on a new feature
that is just being released,

--- Storage Account: Redundant Storage ---

Instructor: So while we're on the subject,
let's talk about redundancy in Azure storage accounts.
So I'm in the process
of creating a storage account in east US.
When I get to the redundancy part of that basic screen,
I'm given four options locally-redundant storage,
geo-redundant storage, zone-redundant storage,
and geo-zone redundant storage.
What does this all mean?
So the concept of redundancy is that by default for free,
Microsoft is going to keep multiple copies of my files
in case of failure in case of emergency.
So even at locally redundant storage, which is the cheapest
and most basic option, Microsoft is gonna keep a total
of three copies of any file I upload
into the storage account.
So the reason it does that is
because hard drives from time to time fail,
they are spinning magnetic discs.
And despite our best efforts in engineering,
after so many thousands of hours of active use,
the hard drive could experience some type of failure.
So Microsoft keeps two additional copies of that file
such that if the hard disk was to fail,
then they're able to reconstruct the hard disc
based on the files they already have stored elsewhere.
The reason they do three
and not two is in the very, very unlikely case
that two hard drives fail, that contain the same files.
So this is giving you a lot of redundancy
and resistance to failures, server rack
and drive failures.
So for locally redundant storage,
you're going to get three copies of your files.
This is stored in a data center.
So I've chosen east US as my region.
This is gonna be stored in one region
of the east US region in one data center.
Now zone-redundant storage would be the next more redundant,
the safer option.
What this does, it still keeps three copies of my files,
but instead of keeping them in the same building,
it spreads them across three different buildings
in the region.
So these are called data centers.
And so zone-redundant storage would protect you in case
that the entire building was to lose power
or to have a flood or some kind of a disaster was to strike.
Your files are safely stored
in a physical separate location.
I should point out that not every
option is available in every region.
So in east US it has these options.
If I go down to north central US,
it's one of the older regions
and we can see that there's only locally-redundant
and geo-redundant.
It doesn't have the concept of zones in north central US.
So as you might expect,
then going to geo-redundant storage is even safer still.
This takes your data out to another region.
So in the case of east US, this could store three copies
of your data in a single data center in east US,
as well as three copies
of your data in a single data center in west US.
So these regions are paired
and so they're basically storing six copies
of your files in two different regions.
Now, geo-redundant storage is also not available
in every region.
So for instance, if you go to Qatar,
then you'll see that you only have the option for locally
and zone-redundant storage.
And then for the last one, geo-zone redundant storage.
That is the safest option of all.
It's going to combine the two.
It's gonna keep three copies
of your files in three different data centers in a region
and three additional copies
of your files in three data centers in the other region.
There are costs associated with this.
So when we go to the costing screen, we can see,
I'll go back to east US.
That LRS is costing in that range
of 2.10 cents per gigabyte.
But if I go to a zone-redundant storage,
that is now 2.60 cents per gigabyte.
And I want to go to geo-redundant storage
prices could change of course, but 4.6 gigabytes.
You see we've now doubled the cost
and geo-zone redundant storage.
The last thing I would say about storage is when you get
into the geo-redundant storage area,
when you've got two regions,
you have the additional checkbox that allows you
to have a read-only access to the second region.
So if I was to check this checkbox,
I would have a second URL
that I could read data from the storage account.
So in the case of east US,
I know that the data's also stored in west US
and I would have a read-only URL
for the west US data in case of emergency.
And of course that does have an impact on pricing too.
So when I go to read access geo-redundant storage,
then I'm going up to 5.90 cents per gigabyte.
I believe it was 4.6 without that second option.
So an extra one
and a half cents almost for this option.
So redundancy really just makes it much more difficult
for Microsoft to lose your files.
The higher you up you go, the more you pay.
But then the more you would expect your files
to be perfectly fine
and survive for millions of years in Microsoft Azure,
as long as you pay for it.

It's called marked for deletion.

So with this setting, and it's on by default,

The reason this is good

--- Storage Account: Encryption ---

Instructor: Okay, we're almost done here.
We're onto the second to last tab,
which is encryption.
Now the big decision here is who are you going to trust
to manage the encryption of your data
inside your storage account?
So all data is encrypted by default,
and if you don't change the setting,
Microsoft will encrypt your data.
This is called Microsoft-managed keys.
It's also Transparent Data Encryption, TDE.
What that means is that you don't get to interact
with the keys at all.
You don't have access to them.
You upload your files unencrypted
and Microsoft will store it on the physical hard disk
encrypted, and then when you go to retrieve the file,
it will automatically decrypt it for you
and you'll receive it unencrypted.
So that is a Microsoft managed key,
and it's the easiest thing to handle.
Now, slightly more complicated
is the concept of customer-managed keys.
Some companies insist on having the security keys
access to them.
Maybe they have a policy for changing them
every so many months.
And so customer-managed keys means that you create
and manage the keys.
Microsoft still uses them transparently.
So you provide the file unencrypted,
it encrypts it using your keys.
The way it does this is it stores the key
in what is called a key vault.
Key vault is a special Azure service for handling secrets,
keys, and certificates.
And so you can create a key
using the key vault and reference that key
when it comes time to creating your storage account.
So it's not too difficult to create a key in the key vault.
And then from that point forward,
Microsoft will use that key.
The default is Microsoft-managed keys,
and unless you really do wanna manage your keys
and take responsibility for that,
then I would suggest you select that.
Now, if you do go the way of customer managed keys,
additional challenge for you
is going to be what services are going to use
your customer-managed keys.
So is it only for blobs and files,
but you're gonna leave tables and queues
using Microsoft Method,
or is it for all service types?
Again, we're gonna leave this as Microsoft-managed keys.
Note that this option cannot be changed
after the storage account is created.
Do you know what's even better
than doing either Microsoft-managed keys encryption or CMK?
Well, that is doing another layer of encryption.
That is the infrastructure encryption option.
It's disabled by default.
You can enable it,
and what that does is it takes your file,
passes it through this first layer of encryption,
and then it takes the resulting encrypted file
and encrypts that before storing it to the hard disk.
So this is obviously a very secure,
because once they've, let's say someone was to get hold of
that hard disk and they were able to reverse
the encryption on it,
well then they would still only be getting encrypted files
out of that and they would have to reverse it again.
So this is going to make somebody's job
a lot more difficult.
So turning on infrastructure encryption,
if that's what you want, is an option,
doesn't cost anything extra.
It may add a little bit more latency
when it comes to the data that you send to Azure,
finally making it to a disk.
But if you're really mostly concerned
with the data that you're sending to Azure being encrypted,
then this is one pretty good option.

Say Yes.

The purpose of the key is to allow you to switch
to the second key before you regenerate the first.
So, the sequence of events would be,
once you realize this key has been hacked,

you switch all your legitimate programs to the second key,

--- Storage Accounts: Stored Access Policies ---

Instructor: We saw in the video
on shared access signature that we assign permissions,
start date, end date,
perhaps IP address and the allowed protocols.
But once we've created this SAS token, we cannot modify it.
We cannot revoke it.
So you create it, you give it to someone,
and you're pretty much stuck
with having this token out there
unless you invalidate the key.
One way around that is using
what's called a stored access policy.
So a stored access policy is a way
to centrally manage SAS token settings.
It is defined at the container level.
So we go up to...
Under containers, we're looking at first container,
and we right-click on the three three dots,
and we look at Access policy.
So this policy does contain permissions,
a start time, and expiry time,
similar to the way that we would
with a shared access signature.
So I'm gonna call this first policy,
and we're gonna give it Read permissions.
And we're going to choose a one week policy
and say, "OK."
So this, I haven't actually granted anybody any permissions.
I've just created the policy called first policy,
and I'll say, "Save."
Now when I go to the file,
I can go to the one that I uploaded
and I can say, "Generate SAS."
And if I choose the first policy from stored access policy,
it's now prevented me from editing the start
and expiry date and time as well as the permissions.
I can then generate the SAS token with this.
Let's say that I've copied it.
Now if I've given this to somebody
and I want to revoke the permissions,
I'm not only able to do so by invalidating the key,
I can also modify the policy.
So if I go back up to the container
and I go look at access policy,
I can either delete the policy or edit it.
And let's say I want to extend the life of this
for a few more weeks.
So now the permissions,
again, have been embedded in the policy.
So whoever has the SAS token can continue to use it
until the new expiry time.
If I wanted to expire it early,
let's say, I wanna expire it 30 minutes ago,
then I can change the expiry time to a much shorter period.
So now I have editable permissions
on the permissions and the time
without having to regenerate the token
or even contact people.
Now this is best used when you have long lived policies.
You wouldn't do this for just a few hours,
but if you had something that is a six month policy,
that's a long time for something to change.
You need to grant additional permissions
or to change the expired time.
The other benefit, of course, is you can reuse this policy.
So you can grant multiple SAS tokens
pointing to the same policy,
and then modify the start date and end date
and the permissions all at once.
So this is a much more flexible
and useful way of managing SAS tokens if you're gonna use it
to give access to your containers and or your files.

--- Storage Accounts: Entra ID (Azure AD) Access Control ---

Instructor: So let's go back to talking about security.

The other type of access is called Entra ID,

This shifts the authentication from what you have in terms
of the secret key into who you are.

within Azure.

--- Storage Account: Lifecycle Management ---

Instructor: So let's talk about
a few of the cool other features
that storage accounts support
for blobs and other file types.
Let's talk about lifecycle management.
If we go on the Azure portal
on the storage account under Data management,
we'll see an option called Lifecycle management.
Now, lifecycle management,
and I think I've mentioned this before in this course,
is the ability to move data
from a more expensive pricing tier
down to cheaper tiers as the files get older effectively.
So for log files and backup files,
things that have less value to you as they get older,
but you might still want to keep, you can move them
to cooler or colder levels of storage
and save significant amounts of money.
So this, let's look at this.
I'm going to click on Add a rule.
Give the rule a name.
I'm going to call this the Saving Money Rule.
And I have the option of applying the rule
to all blobs in the entire storage account,
or to set some type of filter so that only blobs
in a certain container get applied.
I also get to choose which type of blobs get picked up.
Now, one of the interesting parts
is whether I want this to operate on
the blobs themselves, snapshots,
which we'll talk about in the section coming
in terms of Azure files and points in time recovery
for Azure files or versions of files.
So imagine you've turned on versioning
and over six months, nine months, some of the older versions
become less and less important to you.
You can actually just save money
by storing the older versions in cooler
or colder storage that you need in case of emergency,
but you're not likely to access frequently.
So I'm going to do this based on base blobs
just for this example.
Now what we're setting up here is a rule
that says if the blob was last modified
more than so many days ago, let's say more than 30 days ago,
then what do we want to happen to it?
Now we can just delete it.
So if it's a daily log file,
then maybe you don't need to keep more than 30 days
of daily log files.
And so on the 31st day,
this lifecycle management rule will delete the file,
and that's perfectly fine too.
Or maybe you just want to move it from hot storage
to cool storage,
or even as far as going to cold storage,
if you really don't think you're going to need this file
after 30 days.
Now notice some of the comments here.
I haven't mentioned it before in this course,
but as you get into cool, cold and archive,
there is some minimum charges
that come into play.
So when you move a file into cool storage,
you're going to be charged a minimum of 30 days
at that rate.
So you can't just move a file into cool storage for 24 hours
and then delete the file
and expect to save money just for that one day.
You've got a minimum terms of the charge.
You can still delete the file,
but you'll be charged for the full 30 days.
Cold storage is for 90 days,
and archive storage is going to minimum charge you 180 days
at that lowest rate.
But remember, archive storage can be 5%
of the cost of hot storage.
So you're saving a significant amount of money,
and even if you store it just for a month,
you're still saving money at that level.
So as we can see, lifecycle management is where you go.
If you see this on the exam
and it's asking about setting up a job
to automatically move blobs to save money,
then you should be thinking about lifecycle management
as being the easiest way to do that.

as the destination.

azsjdnewstoragedemo, in this container

Now, if you're VMs are already running an Azure,
this would not be an issue.
You could take this script
and you can use your virtual machines
to connect to this file share in this way.
So we can see how an Azure file share is different
than a container or blob storage.
It has different capacities.
It has the concept of directories.
It can be mounted as a drive letter on a Windows machine
or mounted to Linux or macOS in the way
that external disks are,
and definitely is something that's worth looking into
if you want your files to live in the cloud,
be backed up regularly,
but treated as if it was a file server
running in your local machine.
In the following videos, we're gonna be talking about
some of the other features of file shares in Azure.

--- Azure File Shares Snapshots ---

Instructor: So I just want to create some files

Now, the purpose of this video
is really to show you about the concept of snapshots.
Now, snapshots are point-in-time, read-only versions
of your Azure file share.
This allows you to recover deleted or modified files.
And it's not a separate storage account,
it's actually gonna be your same storage account
that's going to store the snapshots as well.
So the snapshot is created manually,
or you can script it
so that you can run it at your own schedule.
These are stored as incremental backups,
so only when there's changes to those files,
then the snapshot gets updated.
The data can be retained for as long as you need.
And again, this is not a separate storage account;
it's all contained within the same snapshot here.
And once you have a snapshot,
then what you can do is restore
from a specific point in time.
So you can browse the snapshot contents,
copy files back to the active file share.
And there is such a thing
as a previous versions tab supported in operating systems
such as Windows.
So again, this is only for general-purpose v2 accounts
or for premium file shares.
So let's create a snapshot.
I'm in the snapshots operation under the file share,
and I'm gonna say add snapshot.
And I'm gonna say this is going to be previousversions.
And so now that we've created a snapshot,
we can actually go into the snapshot.
We can see the storage account file share
as it existed at the time the snapshot was created.
And if I wanted to restore this version of this file,
I can click this restore and it becomes the recent version.
So if this file has changed, has been deleted,
it's been updated since then,
I just navigate into the snapshot and I can then restore it
or download the specific version.
Now, I should point out that another way to get snapshots
is through backups.

to a Recovery Services vault,
and I can even navigate into the Recovery Services vault.

I can see backup items, Azure Storage,
Azure Files, one item.

So you'll see it even says Snapshot
within the Recovery Services vault.
If I go into the file share under Snapshots,
I can see the snapshot that I created manually,
as well as one that automatically kicked off
to do AzureBackup.
Now, the advantage of the automated backup
is that there's a policy,
and so the policy can say
that every day at 7:30 UTC in this example,
it's going to make a backup of anything
that's changed in the file share,
and it's gonna retain those backups for 30 days.
So I have a backup policy to automatically create snapshots
so that I can do the point-in-time recovery,
not only the ones when I can manually create a snapshot,
but I can also have it scheduled through backup.
So definitely a convenient way to keep copies
of your file share over time.

--- VM Availability ---

Scott: Now, when we were creating the virtual machine,
we came across this availability options option
on the basics tab of the virtual machine creation process,
and we ended up choosing no redundancy required
because it was gonna be a standalone virtual machine.
There was never going to be a second or third
or additional virtual machine to stand beside it
that is identical, so there's just no availability options.
It's going to be no redundancy required,
but let's talk about the other three options
in this dropdown.
The first one I wanna talk about is availability set.
Now, availability set, the purpose of it is to protect
against hardware failures within a single data center.
There are two primarily types of failures
that we're trying to protect against.
One is the unintended failures,
and these are called fault domains.
I'm gonna click create new under the availability set here.
So fault domains, the easy way to remember this
is the word fault, which means that something failed,
so it's either the rack or the power supply
or the network switch.
Something relating to this computer failed,
and when you have, I'm gonna call this avail one,
when you have two or more VMs in availability set,
then Microsoft Azure is able to distribute those
across different racks in the same data center.
This way, the most common types of failures
are protected against, and let's say you have two VMs.
Well, in the normal situation, neither of them will go down,
but in the abnormal situation,
only one of them will go down,
and the other one will still be running.
So we have fault domains.
We only have three options,
so we have one fault domain, two, or three.
This is really how many different physical racks
it's gonna be distributed against,
and so in this particular case, with two,
that means two different racks.
Even if you have four or eight or 12 VMs,
they're all gonna be distributed like six and six
on the two different racks.
Update domains is the other thing it protects against,
which are planned maintenance,
so when Microsoft is rolling out updates to its software,
it does it by update domain.
Now, there are, I believe, 20 total update domains,
and so you can imagine Microsoft is just rolling them out,
first to the first one, second to the second one,
third to the third one, et cetera.
So let's say you have five VMs and five update domains.
That means, at any one time, only one of your VMs
will be disturbed by this update.
If you have 10 VMs and five update domains,
that means that two of your VMs will be disturbed
by these updates at a time.
So that's availability set.
Again, protects against hardware failures
within a single data center,
and it's within a single availability zone or single region.
There's no scaling involved.
It's not gonna add more VMs when the demand is required.
It's not gonna remove VMs when you don't need them anymore.
Those are a scale set.
This is just distributing the VMs
across different devices to protect against planned
and unplanned maintenance and failures.
The second option for availability
is called an availability zone,
and this is where you are physically separating your VMs
into different buildings or data centers.
The purpose of this is to protect
against the entire data center failing,
so if I say self-selected zone, you can see I can deploy VMs
into one, two, and/or three.
If I choose all three,
then I'm gonna get three virtual machines to be created.
If I choose just one,
we know exactly which building it's going into,
which is called zone one.
Each of these zones has independent power,
cooling, networking, so even a connection to the ISP
is independent for each of these.
There's nothing shared between them.
It is stronger isolation than availability sets,
so this is even less likely
that your VMs will all go down together.
Now, the third option is the virtual machine scale set.
So this is like an availability zone,
but you also get scaling in addition,
so when you need more servers, more servers get added.
When you don't need as many servers,
you can have servers removed.
So this works either single zone,
or you can deploy this across zones.
It automatically handles the creation of the additional VMs,
it automatically handles load balancing,
there's built-in health monitoring,
and if something was to happen to one of your VMs,
it will replace it.
So this is used when you need auto-scaling and redundancy,
and again, these are for stateless workloads,
so when you're adding VMs and you're removing VMs,
you don't want a customer to be impacted.
You don't want somebody to be in the middle of something,
and then the server they were working on disappears,
so it has to be stateless.
So availability sets, availability zones,
and scale sets protects your application
against both planned and unplanned outages
through different methods.

--- Azure Bastion Service ---

-: So far we've been using what is called the RDP service,
Remote Desktop Protocol to connect to RVM.
We've seen here on the network settings
there's this orange alert symbol next to RDP

this is usually an unsafe practice long term.
You do not necessarily want to have
your administrative ports open to anyone
from any IP address on the internet,
at least even having access to try to log into your device.
So long term should be eliminated completely.
It's for short-term administration purposes only.
At a very minimum, you should probably put

So the Azure Bastion service

This means there's no public IP address required,

--- Virtual Machine Scale Sets (VMSS) ---

Instructor: So when we were dealing
with a single virtual machine,
we saw how easy it was to resize the machine.
If for whatever reason we needed a bigger,
more powerful machine, we could simply make a bigger one.
But that doesn't necessarily increase availability.
It certainly would increase its ability
to handle larger workloads, but it still suffers
from the same challenges in terms of there being,
first of all, a maximum limit
to which you could size a machine.
So we saw that there is a 96 CPU machine.
But if we just wanted to stick with the B series,
for instance, the maximum size is,
looks to be 20 CPUs.
So we could increase the size of this machine
a few more times, but then there'll be a maximum limit.
So another option of this, of course,
is called scaling out.
And that has to do with increasing the number
of identical load-balanced instances there are.
So if we created two B4ms machines,
we would still have all of this power.
We would still be able to handle
usually about twice the workloads,
but you actually reduce the risk
because if one of the machines goes down,
the other machine's still up and can handle the load.
If you go to four or eight
or 16 of these identical machines, you're reducing the risk
of the entire application going down significantly.
In Azure, there is a convenient tool
for you to be able to do this,
and that is called a scale set,
or VMSS virtual machine scale set for short.
So a scale set is a group of identical load balance VMs
that can automatically scale in or scale out.
And scaling in means to reduce the number
of virtual machines, and to scale out
means to increase the number of virtual machines
in that group.
So this is a group of virtual machines acting together,
and they're identical.
Designed for stateless workloads.
So perfect for web servers, perfect for API servers.
Anything where it doesn't need to remember
anything about previous interactions
every time it gets a request.
And because it's a VMSS, it's a scale set,
you're handling everything as a group.
This is a group modifications and group maintenance.
So VMSS is autoscaling, redundancy,
and load balancing for virtual machines.
So I'm in the VMSS section.
I'm just gonna click the Create button here and we'll see.
Now you'll notice it's got a very similar outlook
to creating a virtual machine.
We have additional elements such as health tab,
as well as we can do spot VMs as part of our thing.
We're gonna see all the differences now.
I'm gonna place this in the same virtual machine test group
that we've been working with,
and I'm gonna call this AZ VMSS demo.
Put that in the same region, same availability zone,
the same information about these things
applies as in virtual machines.
Now when it comes to orchestration,
that's where we get to be a little bit different.
So orchestration mode has the choices
between flexible and uniform.
With uniform orchestration, all the instances are identical.
And it's basically the same image, the same configuration.
It's all managed by the same scale set.
It supports autoscaling, rolling upgrades,
automatic healing, and again,
ideal for the stateless workloads like a web server.
So with uniform orchestration,
VMSS handles the entire lifecycle of starting,
maintaining and ending those instances.
You cannot log into an individual instance
and make changes to it.
And if you do make a change, then it could be,
could be overwritten because the VMSS assumes it's the one
that's managing the instance.
Flexible, by the name implying,
is more complicated and can handle stateful workloads.
So the VMs can be individually managed.
You can mix VMs of different sizes.
You could have spot VMs and regular VMs working together.
It still supports autoscaling to a degree,
but there is more manual control in the flexible option.
You can SSH or RDP into individual VMs.
You can make changes that will persist.
And it's great when you have multiple apps or custom apps,
and certain servers have certain functions,
and other servers have more than that function.
And this also works with availability zones.
So if we need to deploy individual VMs
to an individual availability zones,
you're in the flexible state.
All right, I'm gonna choose for the simplicity of it
the uniform model.
We'll have two instances as our default here.
Now, we haven't talked about scaling.
I have a video talking
about manually scaling and autoscaling.
So we'll talk about that in that video.
And just like choosing a VM, we'll choose the image,
we'll choose the architecture, we'll choose the size,
and then we have to give it administrator,
user ID and password.
We have hybrid benefit as an option.
Now, spot instances, we haven't talked about it yet,
but spot instances allow you to bring on capacity
at a discounted rate for workloads
that can tolerate interruptions.
So at any time, Azure can pull back your access
to a spot instance.
It really is like taking a standby seat on an airplane.
You are there because they have the capacity,
but as soon as they don't have the capacity,
you're gonna have to give up your seat.
And then these are the types of evictions.
If you do choose spot instances,
then you have to figure out what to do when you get evicted.
Now, just like with VMs,
you're gonna choose the operating system image,
the premium SSD as the disc type,
what kind of security keys get managed,
same as everything.
There is a virtual network.
Of course, every VM needs one.
And this is choosing to create a new virtual network.
I could add it to one of my existing virtual networks
if I wanted to.
Actually, I'll do that.
I'm gonna put this
in the backend of my existing virtual network.
You'll see that a network interface card is created.
Now, this load balancing,
if you have an existing load balancer,
then this is where you would select that.
Of course, a VMSS requires a load balancer
because you have multiple identical VMs
and they need somewhere to direct the traffic to them.
We'll skip choosing a load balancer for now.
We'll deploy these as standalone VMs.
Same management type options, Defender for Cloud.
How instances get upgraded
in terms of operating system updates,
I'll choose automatic.
I'll turn off boot diagnostics.
These are all.
Now, VMSS has the higher chance of getting terminated
because you get an instance created
and in an instance is destroyed.
So do you need those type of notifications
to some application running on the VM
so it can do something before it gets terminated?
All right.
Overprovisioning is a cool feature for VMSS.
Right now, when we're gonna select the scaling options,
it's waiting until the instance gets to a certain threshold.
Let's say you're waiting on 70% CPU utilization,
and then it creates a VM,
there might be some startup that is required.
You know, scripts need to be installed.
Software needs to be downloaded.
It might take several minutes
for a virtual machine to be instantiated.
And so overprovisioning has a VM on standby
that if you know you're gonna need to add a VM,
pretty surely eventually,
it's got them already spun up and waiting to go.
So this is sort of early provisioning.
You are not billed for the extra VMs,
and they do not count towards your quota.
That's pretty clever.
Now, I mentioned that VMSS has a health feature.
This means that you can have the VMSS
monitor the health of those machines
and do something when the machine is not responding.
So let's say you've got five virtual machines
and one of them is just, you send a request over a port,
and you get zero response back.
Well, then it might be smart
to shut that machine down and restart it.
Or, at the very minimum,
kick that machine out of the rotation.
So you go down from five to four
because you've got one unhealthy instance.
So health monitoring is actually pretty important
for virtual machine scale sets.
Now, typically, what you're doing
for something like an HTTP monitor
is you're looking for a webpage.
So there might be a /status.html page that it would pull up.
And if that returned successfully,
then we know that the machine is healthy.
You can also do a TCP health monitor
where it just checks the port 80.
When you open the port, it opens successfully
and then you can close the port.
So simply checking the port is one other way to do that.
So I'm gonna leave that turned off for now.
Once you've got your health monitoring set up,
you can set it up for automatic repairs.
So again, once we find a bad instance,
what do we do?
We restart the machine.
Do we just instantiate it and instantiate a whole new one?
Do we reinstall the whole image?
There's lots of options for automatic repairs.
Going to Advanced, it used to be very sensitive
that you would have a maximum of a hundred instances,
and you needed to do something special
in terms of a placement group to get more than a hundred.
And as you can see, you don't even have the option.
You can scale beyond a hundred now.
Similar to VMs, you can add extensions,
add applications, have custom data
and user data pass along with it.
These are all the same settings for VMs.

--- VMSS Manual Scaling ---

Instructor: All right, so let's talk
about manually scaling this virtual machine scale set.
So if we go under availability and scale, under scaling,
we can see that we have a slider here.
It's on the manual scale setting.
And we can just pull this slider.
If we had capacity for hundreds,
we could pull the slider all the way to 1,000 instances.
But even going from, well,
let's just overwrite the number and say two to three.
If I was to click save at this point,
then we would have the VMSS
automatically deploy a third VM.
And so we'll see that on the instances tab.
Now, this is not generally the recommended way
to do scaling.
You'd wanna set up auto scale,
which we'll talk about in a second,
to do it based on some schedule
or based on some other metrics
or some prediction in advance.
But simply having multiple virtual machines
that are always running,
it's quite normal as well.
So if you know that your web server
always requires five VMs
and you're never going up or down,
you would be on the manual scale.
And if you did need to save a bit of money, you go down.
If you did need to increase the capacity, you go up.
That's perfectly fine as well.
So this is for predictable workloads.
If I click over to the instances tab and I refresh this,
I'm now running three instances, zero, one and two.
Now, it's worth pointing out as well
that you may not even want to use the portal
for doing the scaling.
You may wanna do things by script.
And so opening up,
I'm gonna open up the cloud shell here to demonstrate.
But we have the ability
to run either PowerShell or CLI commands
to do the scaling for us.
I'm gonna switch over to a Bash shell.
And if I paste in this command,
az vmss scale,
give it the name,
give it the resource group capacity down to two,
and hit enter,
it's now doing the scaling.
Let's minimize this window a little bit.
You'll see that the instance is being deleted.
It says deleting.
And now the instance has been removed from our list.
And so we can see
that there's scriptable ways of changing it, of course.
Now, for the exam,
it's important to remember
the difference between manual scaling and automatic scaling,
and as well as understanding that for manual scaling
is for predictable workloads.
You are gonna pay for the instances that you create,
and you're going to save money
on the instances that you remove.
And so this is an important method
for controlling costs in the cloud,
as well as keeping performance for your users
to the expected standard.

--- VMSS Auto Scaling ---

Tutor: So we've seen that with manual scaling,
that we're able to either increase the performance
that our users experience or reduce the cost
using manual scaling,
but Autoscaling takes that
and makes it automated, of course.
So this is automatically adjusting
the number of VM instances,
it's based on either a schedule or performance metrics,
and whatever you do is based on a predictive event.
So for this AZ 104 video,
we're going to talk about Autoscaling.
Now, you'll see that it's given in a name
and it's gonna place this Autoscaling
as its own resource in the Resource group.
Now, Predictive autoscaling is a relatively new feature
that uses machine learning
to forecast when your app is likely to need more capacity,
and so it knows that every Monday at 10:00 AM
there's a spike in traffic,
and so by 9:45, it's got the server,
additional servers ready to go.
And so you can have the Enable the learning mode
where it's just basically forecasting
when the scaling is gonna be required,
or you can just turn it on
and trust that its predictions can be accurate.
So we're not gonna get into that,
but Predictive autoscaling is possible.
Now what we're gonna do is we're gonna define a set of rules
that when to add a particular instance
or remove an instance.
So, the rule that we're gonna create
is gonna be scaling based on a metric.
And so we're going to say we want a Minimum 2 instances,
a Maximum of 5 instances,
and 2 is the Default, okay?
So we've got this Minimum/Maximum concept,
and when the VMS' first starts up, how many it's created.
So we do need to create these things called rules,
and that's going to indicate when do you add an instance
and when do you remove an instance?
And you should have a rule for both, right?
So, a rule for adding, and at least one rule for removing.
So we can see, for instance,
we want to look at the Percentage of CPU in this instance,
so I'm going to, we're seeing here
that this server is not really pushing the CPU very high,
but let's create the rule based on a 70% CPU,
over a period of 10 minutes,
so that's quite a long period to wait to act,
but we're gonna leave it with the default,
and we're going to increase the count by 1,
so it'll go from 2 to 3,
if the CPU utilization on the 2
will exceed 70% for more than 10 minutes,
and then it's gonna wait 5 minutes
before evaluating this again,
so that's the rule.
Now remember, we've added a scaling out rule,
we should probably add a scaling in rule.
So I'm gonna say if it's less than 25%
for more than 10 minutes, oh, let's make it 15%,
then it's going to decrease the count by 1.
So we've, if it's over 70, we increase,
if it's less than 15, we decrease.
Actually, if we Default to, well, 4,
then the Autoscaling in rule will kick in eventually
and get us back down to 2.
So, I'm gonna say Save.

--- ARM Templates Capture a Point in Time ---

Instructor: So, we're wrapping up this section
on ARM templates and we've seen
that we can capture an ARM template
when we're creating the actual resource
in the Azure Portals.
Then, we can download a template.
We can modify that template.
