Using template specs to deploy it.
And even the command line
with the new AzResourceGroupDeployment cmdlet.
And all that stuff is a way that you can create
ARM templates and use them for automation.
I showed you in the last video,
that the deployments get logged
and basically they'll end up here
in the deployment section of the resource group.
Now, I wanna point out a couple of limitations to this.
So, first of all, I believe there is a maximum number
of deployments that will be logged here.
So, if you are deploying
to the same resource group frequently,
let's say you are deploying it every six hours
and you have this for years,
then there's going to be basically a max limit
to the number of things that will be listed here.
And not every deployment
contains every resource in the resource group.
So, I could create a deployment
that only deploys a network interface card,
and that would be separate than the other two deployments
and there would be no unified ARM template
for everything that's in this resource group, right?
So, each deployment is incremental.
Another issue is that the deployments
capture a moment in time.
So, I deployed this at close to 5:00 p.m. today.
Now, if I go to, let's go to the virtual machine.
So, this is the VM here.
Now this VM is a standard B1s, right?
A very small VM.
Let's say I was going to the size setting
and I decided to upgrade to the B2s, right?
Twice the CPUs, twice the RAM,
twice the temp storage, twice the fun.
So, I'm gonna click Resize.
Now again, behind the scenes, this is using ARM
to make this change, but it's not actually deploying
an ARM template,
but it is using the Azure Resource Manager model
to communicate with the virtual machine.
All right, so, we have upsized the VM from B1s to B2s.
Now, if I go back into the resource group
and I go back into deployments.
First, you'll notice that the resize
did not get stored as a deployment,
so that any kind of settings change that you do
is not actually going to be captured as a deployment.
And the second thing is,
if we go into one of these deployment files,
we can see what happened,
we can see how long it took, and we can see the templates.
So, here is the template.
But what we don't get, so this is the parameters.
And what we don't get is we don't get the new options.
So, if you make a change to a virtual machine
or any other resource,
and you go back into the deployment
that you previously made,
the parameters still show the original settings.
So, this is not a way of capturing
the current state of Azure,
the current state of your resource group.
This captures a point in time.
So, the day and time that you deployed this template,
that's the template that you can make a copy of.
What you can't do within the Azure Portal
is create a brand new ARM template
based on the new settings of that virtual machine.

--- ARM Custom Script Extensions ---

Narrator: Now I've mentioned
that when you deploy a virtual machine,

or virtual machine with the operating system
and no additional software.

to get this additional work
is using something called a Custom Script Extension.

the traditional JSON ARM templates,
and we're looking at ways that we can pass parameters to it
for it can run a script
and do some extra work on creation.

and I am looking for the samples
that contain the words custom script using ARM.
And you'll see one of the results is Multiple Windows of VMs
with custom-script.
There's lots of others, Ubuntu,

of deploying multiple Windows virtual machines
in an ARM template with a script that gets run.

but I want to go to GitHub and say Browse code.
And I'm going to choose the azuredeploy.json file
from GitHub.
And we can see here that it's a JSON file, of course.

we can see a traditional virtual machine deployment.
You got storage accounts, public IP addresses,
network security groups, virtual networks and subnets,
network interface cards,
and, finally, the virtual machines.

that is deploying multiple virtual machines,
you have this copy parameter as part of it
that's going to create multiple versions of this resource.
Okay.

Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachines/extensions.

for whatever you want it to do.

that will do some kind of work when this VM gets deployed.

that multiple VMs get this.

Another cool thing to show you on the deployment side are what's called deployment slots.
Now, by default, when you create a web app, you have a single slot which is production.
And so whenever you deploy code into your web app, it goes straight live there.

So I'm going to say add.

And I'm going to have a staging spot which is the production URL with this staging appended.
We'll let that run.
So now I have two places to deploy my code.
So you can set up your continuous deployment to deploy to staging not to production.
Or you can do your publish profile within Visual Studio to publish to staging.
And when you manually push the publish button it goes here.
Then you can go to this URL and you can double check that it's okay.
Do a pretest before production sees it.
And then when you're ready let's say you've tested staging.
It's good.
There's no bugs.
You're happy.
You simply just hit swap and swap will take the staging code, push it to production and take the production
code and push it to staging.
So it's going to basically swap them exactly as it's called.
What this allows you to do then, is to stage your deployments by going first to a testing spot, testing
it, then when you're happy manually going to production, and then if something was to go wrong, let's
say you were not happy with something in production after doing this process, then you can swap it
back.
You have the old version of the code standing by in case you need it, and you don't have to deploy
the old version of the code in order to get that updated.
So this is a way of safely deploying code and safely rolling back the code if something was to go wrong.
No extra deployments required, it's just swapping the URLs.
Another cool feature is this concept of traffic percentage, which some people would call a B testing.
So let's say you want 10% of your traffic to go to the new code, 90% of the traffic to go to the old
code.
You could simply set up this 9010 traffic percentage.
And Azure will automatically send 10% of the traffic to this new area.
So this is a way of doing a small scale test in production with only some of your users for them to
see the new site.
Maybe you want 1% or 5% or 25%.
Then you can determine if there's any issues.
If you get tickets or you're getting phone calls or something's going wrong without affecting everybody
all at once.
So these are some of the features you get with web apps that you don't have with virtual machines without
a lot of manual effort And so the deployment options are some of the better options for using web apps.

make it into a higher level plan is called scaling up.

up.

test or production.

What I'm running under is a legacy plan standard S1.
Now if I simply wanted to get more CPU, more memory, but I was otherwise satisfied with the level
of performance, I could simply just upgrade to the S2 plan and I would get double the CPUs, double
the memory, same number of storage in terms of gigabytes, same limitation in terms of maximum instances
and same SLA promise.
Actually, if we go under the feature set, we can sort of see some of these other limitations.

about in a second.
Only ten daily backups.
Only five deployment slots.
And the zone redundant option is not available to me.

I just say select and it's going to do that upgrade for me and that's been seamless.
So.
So within those seconds, it's now running on an S2 plan and hopefully my users never even noticed the
switch.
What they should notice is the increased performance.
Now likewise, if S2 is too powerful and I'm not using it to its full potential, I can also downgrade
to back to S1 if I so desired.
And that's pretty simple.

roughly double the cost.
All right.
So that's pretty straightforward in terms of scaling up.
Now there are limitations of course.
So once you've gone from S1 to S2 to S3 and you've got the four CPUs and the seven gigabytes, well

US a month.
Now, there's a problem here in that you end up with this single instance and a single point of failure.

Your users are going to notice the problem.
So that's the limitation of scaling up is a maximum size for this.

--- Azure Container Registry (ACR) ---

Instructor: So we saw when we were creating
the container instances and the container apps
that we had the option of pulling the image
from a registry that we control
as opposed to the sample image.
And one example of that would be
the Azure Container Registry.
So Azure Container Registry is basically
a private container that lives in Azure
that you can store your images.
It's like a code repository, but you're not storing code.
You're storing compiled images for your containers.
It supports versioning, it supports types of security,
granting access to certain people to it.
Again, this is a private repository.
You have the choice of setting the particular
level of privacy that you want on your images.
It allows you to have tasks that do automated builds
and automated deployments.
Once you push something into the registry,
it can actually cut off some tasks.
So I'm here to create a container registry.
I'm putting it in my resource group,
I'm giving it a name, giving it a location.
Container registry does support availability zones,
which means that it gives you an additional layer
of availability if one particular data center
in a region is down.
If you've deployed this into several zones,
you would have a redundancy.
That's only available on the premium plan.
We have basic, standard, and premium options.
We can see from the Microsoft Learn website,
basic is more like a developer option.
It comes with a low amount of storage
and it's really for lower-usage scenarios.
Standard is the same as basic except it has higher
amount of storage and can pass through more images
in terms of its bandwidth.
It should satisfy most production scenarios.
And the premium registry has the highest amount of storage,
highest amount of concurrent operations,
and it also has geo replication so that you can have
a single registry across multiple regions,
and you can even use private links
so that it doesn't even have a public URL.
You have to set up this point-to-point connection
to restrict access to the registry,
and as we saw, then you could use
the availability zones as well.
So for the purpose of this demo,
I might as well just pick a basic registry.
Oh, in order to get this as a proxy
onto a network where I can have private access
versus public access, I do need to be
on the Premium SKU.
Customer Managed Key is only available on the Premium SKU.
And then I can save, review, and create.
So like I said, this is just like a container.
There's a certain amount of storage.
It can handle a certain amount of transactions
per second, et cetera, but it's tied
towards container images.
So I'm gonna say go to resource.
So on the basics here, you can see
I have 10 gigabytes to work with.
Obviously a low amount.
That would be restrictive if I wasn't
only just doing this for development.
So in the next video, I'm going to show you actually
how I will push an image into the repository
and pull an image out of the repository
as a demonstration of what a container registry can do.

--- Introduction to VNET Peering ---

1
Now when you're setting up
2
a complex production environment with multiple networks,
3
multiple servers, multiple applications,
4
sometimes you do need
5

8

16

17
between the different subnets.

19

22

23

24

25
one virtual network to communicate with virtual machines
26

27

28

--- Azure DNS Services ---

Tutor: So let's do a quick recap of what the DNS is.
DNS stands for Domain Name System,
and this is the way that domain names
that we may remember
are turned into IP addresses.
So if you enter microsoft.com into your browser,
that resolves to this IP address,
and you don't have to remember that address.
The other important thing is, of course,
the address can change.
Let's say you wanna move servers
and so you create a new server with a new IP address
and all you have to do is update the directory
to give the new address,
and then people will start going to that machine over time.
There is some complexities to the global DNS system
and how things are cached,
and things are not updated instantly around the world,
and things like that,
but we don't have to worry about that for now.
So the DNS resolves human-memorable domain names
to non-memorable IP addresses.
Within Azure, you have a number of options,
you can obviously provide your own DNS services
hosted elsewhere, maybe your registrar,
maybe on your own premises.
You can use the Private DNS Services within Azure,
we'll talk about that.
You can use a public DNS service that we'll talk about,
and there's even a domain, app domains,
which we'll talk about.
So the Bring Your Own DNS
or the externally hosted domain name server
is basically the custom domain,
so it could be mycompany.com,
gets resolved to an IP address
before that traffic reaches Azure.
So the user enters yourcompany.com into their browser
and some other service, which could be your registrar,
could be hosted at your own site, does that work,
and then the traffic that comes to Azure,
already has that resolved, pretty straightforward.
Now the total opposite of that is the Private DNS,
where you have a virtual network inside of Azure
and you wanna resolve things to various domain names,
but it's not publicly accessible.
And so you can basically,
that's the key to know about that
is you have servers within your own environment
that may have their own names for things,
and it's not available to anyone outside of Azure.
Now, I like to say there are no rules,
there are rules, but there are basically no rules,
you don't have to register this custom domain
with any registry.
That means, if you wanna be cheeky as the British say,
you can use microsoft.com
as your own private DNS,
you can name your servers,
database.microsoft.com,
website.microsoft.com,
something else .microsoft.,
inside your Azure Private Network.
Again, it doesn't affect anything outside of your network,
so what that does is it actually might break some things
because if your web server wants to go to microsoft.com
to do an update, for instance,
then you've overridden the DNS with your private DNS.
That is more commonly done for
dev.local, production.local, staging.local,
you can create labels for servers
that only have private IP addresses
and don't have public IP addresses.
The Public DNS, it's very similar to the Bring Your Own DNS,
it's just working like your register would,
but it's available within Azure.
We have a video coming up
talking about these things, demonstrating them.
And finally, there is something called
the Azure Domain Services,
where you can, if you need a domain,
you can pay money for a custom domain,
yourcompany.com if it's available,
and associate that with a an Azure App Service.
So those are the options
currently within Microsoft Azure relating to DNS,
in the next couple of videos,
I will demonstrate to you private, public,
and also the domain services.

.com, .net, .org, and .info,

It's not gonna work.

--- NSGs ---

Instructor: So you may have seen
when we were setting up virtual machines in the past,
there's a concept within Microsoft Azure
called network security groups.
Now, network security groups or NSGs
are a core way to control network access in Azure.
They act like a firewall,
using allow or deny rules based on things
like IP address port and protocol.
NSGs can be associated with either the subnet
or the network interface card or NIC.
And each rule includes a priority
and the rule with the lowest priority number
that matches will win.
So I'm in the Azure portal
and I've searched for network security groups.
I'm going to click create
and I've chosen a subscription
and you can choose a resource group.
I'm gonna put this into a ZVM, an existing resource group.
Now I'm gonna give it a name.
So in this case I'm gonna call it demo NSG.
And we have to choose a region.
Now this region should be the same as your subnet
or your virtual machine that you want
to associate this NSG with.
We don't need to deal with tags.
So we're creating a default basic NSG.
We're gonna say review and create and then create.
Now like I said, the network security group
acts like a firewall.
It's what's called an access control list.
And it contains both inbound
and outbound rules
that which traffic is allowed onto the network
and which traffic would be specifically denied.
Now you'll see from this that there are a set
of default rules.
So we already have three rules on the inbound
and three rules on the outbound that is given to us
that we can't control.
Let's look at them, I'm gonna go under settings
under inbound security rules to start,
and I'm gonna minimize this menu.
So we can see that there are three rules.
They are given the highest priorities.
So typically the rules that you create
will have lower numbers, thus higher priorities.
And they are that all traffic
from the virtual network is allowed.
All traffic from a load bouncer is allowed
and all other traffic is denied.
So if you have a network that's associated
with a default network security group
that you have not modified,
it's only gonna allow traffic from the virtual network
and the load bouncer.
It's not gonna allow any other traffic onto that network.
That is a access control list.
And you can see it's got port, protocol, source,
IP address or source destination address and the action.

Okay? So that rule takes priority.
Alright, so that's an overview of NSGs.

or to a network interface card.
Azure will evaluate both sets of rules together.
So if you have a NSG assigned to your subnet

--- Introduction to Load Balancing ---

Scott Duffy: So, in this set of videos,
we're gonna talk about a very interesting concept
and that is the concept of load balancing.
Now, you may have heard this term
and maybe you're intimately familiar with it,
but let's talk about it.
So, a load balancer is generally,
a device that usually evenly distributes traffic
that's coming in from somewhere
to multiple servers that are behind the load balancer.
So, you can think of the load balancer
like a traffic cop
or like somebody standing at the front of the passport line,
directing people that come to a specific person
that can help them.
So, you go to line number one, you go to line number two.
As the traffic comes in, there's a load balancer
to make sure that the lines are evenly distributed.
We can look at that in a technical way
with a graph like this.
So, in this graph we actually have two load balancers.
Traffic is coming in from the internet over port 80,
at the top.
Then we have our first load balancer called
the public load balancer.
We'll talk about that in a second.
And there are three web tier servers
behind the public load balancer.
And so the traffic comes in and is distributed to them
and then those servers themselves need to communicate
with some type of business tier.
And there's an internal load balancer
to distribute traffic over port 443,
between the web tier and the business tier.
So, as you can see,
there's a lot of redundancy built in this system.
If anything happens to one of these servers,
the system will continue to function.
So, when you're dealing with critical workloads,
a single virtual machine often will not suffice.
So, either you have a lot of traffic
and so you need to have multiple aisles open
to serve people,
or it is too important for there to be any closures.
So, if you think about public websites like microsoft.com,
the public facing website from Microsoft,
there are hundreds and thousands of servers
that are serving up the content from that site
because it's just too important for a single server
to be able to be responsible for it.
But if you have a non-critical workload,
let's say you have a server
that just runs a job once a month, you know,
collects the files from one directory
and copies them to the other,
if that's a single server's job,
then you don't necessarily need all that redundancy
just for this non-critical workload.
So, what kind of options do you have in the cloud
when you need more?
So, option number one
is that there's always this scale up option.
So, you can take the virtual machine that you're using
and you can find a bigger, more powerful virtual machine.
Microsoft Azure and all the cloud computing platforms
have a lot of options.
We saw earlier in this course
over 1000 virtual machine options
when you go to create a virtual machine.
So, if you're currently running a D2 instance
with eight gigs of ram and two virtual CPUs,
you can upgrade to the D4 instance with 16 gigs of ram
and four CPUs for double the price.
Or you can go up to eight or 16 or 32.
There are options to go higher
and the pricing is just purely double
when you double the power.
You can see it goes on beyond 32 all the way up
in this case to 96.
So, you can go up pretty high in terms of scaling up,
but this is not still the recommended way of scaling
when you have the need
for additional support for a busy server.
Now one of the conditions here
is when you reach that D96 server, you've got nowhere to go.
Okay, there's no D 128
or D 256 that's available.
There might be in the future,
but you're already running a very big powerful server.
So, there are a few downsides of using a single server
and I alluded to them before.
It is true in computers and technology
that sometimes you do need to reboot.
So, you do have a operating system update,
a security update,
the Azure itself needs to be updated
or on your side you have an application
that might not even require the operating system to reboot,
but you have to stop traffic
in order to upgrade an application to a new version
and quickly test it before you turn on traffic again.
So, either Microsoft Azure
or your own updates may require server downtime.
There's also this unexpected downtime
in terms of power outages, internet outages and others.
So, you could have a single rack
or the entire data center going offline
for a couple of hours
and that would be inconvenient to you as well,
as well finally, you may have a what's called a burst,
which is a in short, infrequent need for more power.
And so there's other ways of doing scaling
in terms of load balancing,
that are more convenient for traffic
that comes in suddenly
as opposed to having to go from a D4 server up to D64
and then back down to a D4
because you're getting one hour of unexpectedly high amount
of traffic into your application.
So, that was option one is just to scale up and down.
Option two is scaling out and in and out to be more precise.
So, this is the diagram we saw
at the beginning of this video
where you have three servers at the web tier
and three servers at the business tier.
It would be very easy to add a fourth or a fifth
or a sixth server to either of those tiers
if the demand for your application was to rise.
You're also going to see less risk
in terms of sudden downtime
and it's easier to even bring your application down
for Azure system updates or Windows updates
'cause you can do it one server at a time.
I did mention public and internal load balancers.
So, a public load balancer faces the public internet.
It accepts traffic over port 8443
or a public port
and then it distributes it to the backend VMs
by using the public,
load balancer has a public IP address
and this is often used for websites and web apps and APIs,
anything that is public facing.
The internal load balancer as the name applies,
accepts traffic within the virtual network.
It does not accept public traffic,
does not have a public IP address
and this is used for backend servers,
internal services like databases
or the middle tier of an application.
So, I mentioned before, if you do your scaling this way
with two or more servers behind a load balancer,
you get benefits and one of them is the uptime availability
of your entire application.
We saw before if one of those three servers went down,
application would still function,
even if two of those servers went down,
the application could still function.
Now maybe it would be slower or less performant,
but you could suffer minor outages
and still have uptime.
Microsoft provides what are called service level guarantees
or SLAs,
in terms of their promise for your virtual machine.
So, if you're using a single standalone virtual machine
without a load balancer,
and you choose one of the premium
or premium ultra discs,
they do have a uptime guarantee of 99.9%
for the premium SSD
in which you would get a 10% credit in a month
if they fail to meet that.
If it's worse and it's only 99% uptime, you get 25% credit.
And if in some disastrous way they only have 95%
or less uptime in a month
for a single standalone virtual machine,
they'll give you your money back for that month
for that virtual machine.
So, even without the load balancer,
you do get some type of service level agreement.
But when you start to use a load balancer
in particular an availability set,
then you are getting not just 99.9% guaranteed,
you're getting 99.95% guaranteed,
which may seem like a small jump of just 0.5%,
but that cuts your expected downtime in half
and you can see for 99 and 95,
as that remains in terms of your service credit.
When you're using zones,
two or more VMs in availability zone,
again that has to be probably behind a load balancer
or some type.
Then the uptime expectation goes to 99.99%,
which is a massive increase in the availability
for that service.
Obviously the more VMs you add,
then your expectations
should be to improve upon that even further.
Now within Microsoft Azure there are three
or four types of load balancers.
There is the Azure load balancer service.
So, the fact that it's named load balancer,
might make it a little confusing
when I talk about other types of load balancers.
There's another load balancer called an application gateway
and we'll talk about the difference of that in a second.
And finally, we have the front door service,
and that is a whole range of services bundled together
that is probably the recommendation
if you're looking at a very web facing property
and you want a variety of things like CDN
and load balancing across regions and things like that.
Front door service is probably going to be my default answer
unless there's a really good reason not to.
Like if there's a cost factor,
then you might choose one of the other services.
But if money is not mentioned in the question,
I might be leaning towards front door services being
what Microsoft recommends.
Now for the basic load balancer, there are different SKUs.
You may hear the basic SKU
and previous versions of this course, I mentioned it,
that has been deprecated.
So, you cannot create a basic SKU anymore from scratch,
although they still exist
and you'll never see this as being an answer on the test.
The standard SKU is now the option.
It has the service level agreement,
so it has its own service level agreement
on top of the virtual machines.
It supports a network address translation in terms of NAT,
you can do private links, it can do availability zones
and things like that.
For virtual network gateways there's another type
but that's not covered on this exam.
Now I've mentioned we saw with virtual machines
and availability set,
you get a certain amount of uptime guaranteed.
In an availability zone situation,
it's the most you can have in terms of availability.
So, if availability is your primary concern in the question,
then I would look for the availability zone answers,
and that could be standard load balancer.
That could also be application gateway.
So, when you're creating these load balancers
in an availability zone,
you're going to get a couple of options
to choose from when you're creating it.
One is what's called zonal,
Z-O-N-A-L,
and that means you're placing the availability load balancer
in a specific zone,
and then you're gonna need more than one
to put it into zone one and zone two and zone three.
So, you're talking about instances
of the load balancer.
For zone redundant,
this means that Microsoft takes care
of distributing it across zones
and that is a different, again, type of load balancer
that you get to choose from.
So, using load balancers allows you to build
what are called highly available multi-zone architectures.
Now I mentioned the types of load balancers.
There's distinction between them as well.
So, this, you're gonna look for this in the question.
And the Azure load balancer
is what's called a layer four load balancer
that operates at the network layer TCP or UDP.
That means it has no knowledge of HTTP protocol.
It cannot detect the domain name or the path.
It's not inspecting the packets,
it just understands TCP and ports.
And so if you need that kind of load balancer,
then you would go with the Azure load balancer.
If you need to understand paths,
let's say the video subdirectory
has its files on one server
and the images subdirectory
has its files on a different server,
that would be an application gateway.
Okay, so that's called a layer seven load balancer.
It operates at the application layer.
Finally, the front door service supports load balancing
as well as SSL offloading
and content delivery network support, things like that.
Now, I haven't mentioned the traffic manager service.
That counts as a load balancer technically,
but all it does is it manages
the Azure DNS
in a way that if the person types the domain name
into their browser,
it goes and does a DNS lookup of that name
and the Traffic Manager service
will give them a different answer
based on their geographical location
or some other algorithm.
So, Traffic Manager
doesn't really operate as a load balancer,
it operates outside of the cloud really,
and it's doing a DNS lookup providing different answers
to different people based on their location.
Anyways, this video got quite long,
but that's a good overview of load balancing
within Microsoft Azure.

So we have successfully deployed the load balancer, three virtual machines, three network interface

setup, and how what I would do to diagnose those.
Now I want to start off by saying that there's really only 4 or 5 setting blades within a load balancer.
We can see them listed as front end, back end health probes, the load balancing rules, and the network
address translation rules.
There's also the outbound rules, but that's not a very rare thing that people are going to set up outbound
rules for traffic.
Okay, so what can go wrong with your front end configuration?
Well, of course, this is the IP address that's available to the world, right?
So if you're going to enter that IP address into a browser, you're going to get some type of result.
It's pretty cool that this template has a running web browser that says hello World and the name of
the server as part of the configuration.
So thumbs up to the template that that does that.
That could be an nginx or something type thing.
So the IP address is what is connected to the world of course.
And so if you're using a domain name, you have to make sure that that domain name resolves to the correct
IP address.
That that's all set up uh, for domain resolution.
Now the IP address is associated with rules.
So this is this will be under load balancing rules.
But if I go under this I can see that it's my HTTP rule.
Okay.
That's what the rule that it's associated with.
I'm going to do that later on here.
Go into the next one is back end pool.
So what can go wrong with your back end pools?
Well first of all are you pointing these load balancer to the correct servers.
And so you may have 123.
You might have several back end pools that are all resolved to answer for this traffic.
You can see here that one of my virtual machines is in a stop state.
I just stopped it myself.
And so one of the things that could be happening, of course, is that only two of your servers are
doing the work, and the third server is never called upon.
Right.
An unequal distribution of traffic.
Now you're not going to see VM two coming up on the web browser because it's stopped and because of
the health probe, health probe is removed VM two from the rotation.
So the health probe is what decides whether the back end pool is returning a normal response.
So this is the health probe.
If we go into it, we see it's a TCP health probe that's checking port 80.
We also have the option for HTTP health probes and HTTP s health probes.
An HTTP health probe checks the document can be retrieved from the web server, whereas the TCP health
probe checks to see if it can connect to the port.
So this is trying to connect to port 80.
Now it's going to try it every five seconds forever.
And if at any time the health probe fails, it's going to remove it from the rotation until the health
probe succeeds.
So if the problem resolves itself, then that's fine.
Go to back end pools.
This server that is deallocated has been kicked out of the pool based on the health rule.
I can actually click on this and say start.
But we're going to start that up.
And then then hopefully we'll see VM start to get responses from our calls.
Now the fourth A critical part of a load balancer course is the load balancing rules.
And we saw the front end IP addresses connected to this rule.
And this rule basically takes the front end address and will send all traffic from port 80 in inbound
to port 80 on the server.
So you're going to have to have make sure that your server is listening on that port.
This is the pool that contains the virtual machines.
So make sure that your load balancer rule goes to the right pool.
Again the health probe has been chosen here.
Now session persistence is whether the same user gets sent to the same server every time, or whether
you can have any server responding to that user.
This concept of Idle is basically the client and the server have this open connection, as opposed to
it being closed and then having to be reopened.
All right.
So we're going to leave the load balancing rules as they are.
The final section is Nat rules.
Now we don't have any setup, but network address translation is more specifically when you're taking
in traffic over one port and sending it to a port mapping to the target port.
It is, you know, incoming traffic sent to a selected IP address and port combination to a specific
virtual machine as opposed to the whole group of them.
So if you if you're looking for that kind of setup, then this is where you're going to debug any kind
of problems with that.
We don't have any of those.
Now if you do need to configure rules for traffic leaving the virtual machines traveling through the
load balancer, then you can set them up in the outbound rules here.
So in terms of debugging your your front end being set up correctly with the correct load balancing
rule, your back end being set up correctly.
Your server's all in a running and proper state.
Your health probes pointing to a proper location that would successfully tell your load balancer whether
or not it is healthy properly.
And finally, the rules themselves that you know, tell you which traffic can get through and which
traffic cannot.
So for instance, right now we don't have an Https rule.
And so that would be basically blocked by the load balancer.
Now I should also mention that you can set up an alerts process so that if there's ever any health probe
issues, uh, back end servers that are not responding, that a particular alert group is going to be
notified or an action group here.
So we can say the resource is the load balancer under the condition we can choose the um, we can see
here some of the signals available health probe status being one the number of bytes uh, being, uh,
sent through the number of packets, whether or not the load balancer, these are more administrative.

I restarted the server.
And number of problems are going to, uh, go up and go down.
Right.

--- Create Application Gateway ---

Instructor: Now, the load balancer
that we created a couple of videos ago
is actually what's called a layer four load balancer,
and it operates on the layer four
of the OSI transport model.
That means that it understands the difference
between TCP and UDP.
It understands IP addresses, NIC cards,
but it doesn't understand applications.
It doesn't have a grasp or a concept
of the packets that are being sent to the server.
It doesn't have the ability to inspect those packets
and make decisions based on the contents of those packets.
It's purely just IP addresses and ports.
Let's say you need the load balancer
to be able to look at the path of the URL
and send traffic to one server
if the URL contains a certain path
and a different server, if the URL is slightly different.
Okay, you could have different domain names
with the same IP address
or just paths underneath that domain name.
Well, then you need what's called a level seven
or a layer seven load balancer.
And in Azure, that's called an application gateway.
So if we go up and we search for application gateway,
we're going to see application gateways.
It is a load balancer,
but again, it operates at a higher level.
It understands that you're dealing with a web app
and it understands, you can look at the contents
of that packet and make decisions based on that.
So I'm going to say create application gateway.
I'm going to put this into the same resource group
that I previously did,

--- On-Premises Backup ---

-: Now I briefly mentioned that the backup service
does not only deal in Azure workloads.
So if we go into the recovery services vault
under getting started backup
we saw the selection includes Azure Stack,
which is an On Premises cloud and Azure Stack Hub,
as well as On Premises itself.
And so choosing On Premises gives us these eight options.
Now files and folders, again, very selective backup.
It can run as an application
on the server that's being backed up.
A couple of different virtual machine options
the Windows Hyper-V, as well as the VMware product
which Azure integrates into very well.
It's a partner.
You can do a On Premises SQL Server backup.
You can do a SharePoint server, an exchange server.
You can back up the system state,
or you can do what is called Bare Metal,
which takes the entire server right
down to the operating system to back that up.
And so you have a number of options.
Each of these options has slightly different instructions.
So if I say files and folders,
and I say prepare infrastructure,
then I'm taken to their recommendation
which is the Recovery Services agent.
Now, the Recovery Services agent is a download
for Windows Server,
or Windows client where you can download this application
install it onto your machine.
You'll need to get credentials
so that your machine can register
to the Recovery Services vault.
The credentials expire after 10 days
in order to set this up.
And then you schedule the backup and you can basically, just
as we did with the VMs, monitor the backup on the jobs page.
We can even receive notifications when backups don't work
in terms of notifications, alerts.
So we can see here files and folders are dealt with one way.
If I don't do that
and I instead choose the Hyper V virtual machine
then we're not given the agent, we're given a backup server.
So this needs to be a machine that doesn't have to
be dedicated to it, but you have to have a machine other
than the the machine being backed up, of course.
And that's called MABS, or Microsoft Azure Backup Server.
This also requires the credentials.
And then you're going to use the MABS UI to
configure the backup to which virtual machines in HyperV
you're going to be backing up.
We can see a VMware also uses MABS.
You do need to...
I guess you have the option of using system center
for some of these backups.
Now, different again, different agents are
gonna require different sets of instructions.
You can see SQL Server.
If we go down
to the Bare Metal option this is also MABS.
Again, this is an external server that runs outside
of the machine that's being backed up.
And so this MABS has replaced a couple
of different backup products that previously existed.
And so you can then configure all of your backups in here.
We also notice how the option of having
more than one thing selected at a time.
And I guess MABS is the default option when
you've got to do lots of different types of backups.
So as you can see On Premises does work
keep in mind for the files and folders one
you could even run this on
a AWS EC2 instance or Google Cloud server.
Anywhere where you can install this agent
on a Windows server, you can actually get a backup
into the Azure Recovery Services vault.

--- Soft Delete for VM Backups ---

NOTE
This file was generated by Descript
Now Azure has released a new
security feature called the Soft
Delete for Recovery Services Vaults.
If you're not familiar with the
term, a soft delete is when you go
and delete something but it doesn't
actually get deleted right away.
There is a delay between the time
that you initiate the delete process
and when it gets actually deleted.
This allows you to change your mind.
Now, the reason this is a security
feature is because if somebody was to,
hack into your account and to delete
your virtual machines, they would then
also maybe want to delete your backups.
And that would really get you in trouble.
So them not being able to affect your
backups because you can restore within
14 day period is a security feature.
Now this is enabled by default on
brand new recovery services vaults.
If you have an older recovery
services vault, you can turn this on.
Now in Azure, the soft delete
process takes 14 full days.
So if you initiated delete,
there is a 14 day period where
it's still available for you.
That means you can undelete it.
And once you undelete it,
that file is available to
recover from things like that.
It becomes back to a normal
state on the 15th day.
If you have not undeleted the
file, it gets auto deleted.
Now that's explained.
This graph is from Microsoft website.
Pretty much that's what's
being explained here is.
After 15 days and no user
action was required and the
data gets permanently deleted.
And if you stop that, undelete within
14 days, then you can actually resume
the backup jobs and get your virtual
machine going again, or you can
restore from that backup, et cetera.

--- Azure Site Recovery / ASR to Site-to-Site Replication ---

Instructor: Now when the worst case happens
and a whole entire region was to be unavailable,
let's say the internet was knocked off
to the US East region.
What do you do?
So one option is to have a disaster recovery plan in place
and to have a backup set of machines
and a backup environment ready to go.
In Azure, this is using the tool
called Azure Site Recovery or ASR.
So the idea here is that we're going
to take our environment that we have,
let's say in this case, in the East US.
We have a couple of virtual machines
in an availability set on a network
that run on a couple of discs.
Of course, virtual machines require discs,
and so you're gonna wanna have ready
to go in a different environment that same setup.
Now, that doesn't mean that it's a hot swap,
where you have these machines running
and they're, you know, they're on,
you're being charged for them,
but they're not being used.
This is a case of where you have it ready to go,
but as you can see in this diagram,
they're not running and they're not ready to go.
What ends up happening is when this environment
is not available, then Azure's going
to deploy these machines in a ready state
and with all of your data backed up
and in the exact same configuration,
and all you need to do is just direct the traffic
to that new environment.
So this is sort of a,
not a hot swap,
but a cold swap.
And it could take, I mean, realistically it could take
10 or 15 minutes for an environment to be ready,
not only creating the virtual machines,
but ensuring that they are in a ready to go state in terms
of the code that needs to be on there and things like that.
So the whole startup in instantiation process.
So it could be a good 10 minutes
before your environment is ready to go,
but that is obviously better than being down for hours.
So that's is the tool called Azure Site Recovery.
So I've got this virtual machine
that I've created and let's show
how to create this disaster recovery setup.
I'm in the portal under the virtual machine
and under the backup
and disaster recovery menu,
there is this disaster recovery setting.
Now, when I go into it, it says,
welcome to Azure Site Recovery.
And since I did not deploy this virtual machine
into an availability zone,
then I don't have the availability zone
recovery option available to me.
You'll see that it's chosen a region for me
to have my new environment.
Obviously, the farther away the region is,
you are going to have increased charges
when it comes to the data transfer
between continents here.
And this might be a slower connection,
so there might be some lag.
So it's generally a better idea to choose a region
that is not too far
from your current region.
Now, there is also this concept of region pairs
where for every region in Azure,
there is one region
that has the highest speed connection.
I believe that North Europe
and West Europe are region pairs.
And so this is going to be the cheapest
and quickest connectivity between these two regions.
And there shouldn't really be any event
that affects the Northern Europe data centers
that also affects West Europe data centers.
So let's choose that.
Now, again, it's up to you.
You could choose
US,
you could choose farther off into Europe,
into Poland, for instance.
That's totally up to you.
Just realize there are cost and leg implications.
So let's say next,
and what we're gonna see here is it's going
to set up under the same subscription,
a brand new resource group, a brand new virtual network.
It's understanding
that I'm doing replication on a single VM.
So you'll see that it's got the ability
to do this against availability zones,
availability sets, and VMSS.
If I had a proximity placement group for my VMSS,
I could choose one.
A lot of this stuff is going to get created,
just not the virtual machines being instantiated.
I could choose capacity reservation if I had any.
Now we can see here that it's also gonna create
the storage account,
and it's gonna do a replication
between the storage accounts.
So from the region in Northern Europe
where my machine currently exists,
it's gonna use a recovery services vault to do backups,
and it's gonna make sure
that the data is ready
to go into the new region.
So now I can say review,
and it's showing me what's gonna happen,
the target and the source,
basically storage, replication, etcetera.
And I can say start.
So as you can see, it's actually quite straightforward,
quite simple to set up this emergency
recovery that's going to now,
it's gonna take some time obviously
to set these things all in place,
but it's quite simple using the portal to get this going.
And if there was, like I said, this rare disaster,
it hasn't happened in at least a year,
I think, maybe more,
where a region is not available.
If that does happen, then I would have another,
another environment in a different region
that I could start up
and it would have all of the data backups
and everything ready to go.

'cause we're still talking about networking

693
we're gonna talk about load balancers.
694
So there are a couple of types
695
of load balancers within Azure,
696
three of them I would say that we'll talk about.
697
One of them is just called the load balancer.
698
It's self-named, load balancer.
699
There used to be a Basic load balancer
700
and that has been deprecated.
701
From this point forward,
702
Standard load balancer is the only option.
703
So this load balancer is what's called
704
layer four load balancing.
705
This means it has no knowledge of HTTP, HTTPS,
706
paths, query strings, nothing like that.
707
It's just network level.
708
It's for basically taking network traffic
709
and taking network traffic in
710
and sending it to a backend,
711
one or more backends in a distributed fashion
712
so that it sits in front of your virtual machines
713
and distributes traffic.
714
Okay, so this is the most basic,
715
again, network level load balancing.
716
There is another load balancer called application gateway
717
and that's called a layer seven load balancer.
718
Now, this is application-aware.
719
That means that if it is an HTTP server
720
or a web server, you can check the domain name
721
and you can say, oh, this domain goes here.
722
That domain goes there.
723
The application gateway is aware of the domain name,
724
it's also aware of the path.
725
So images can go this way
726
and videos can go that way.
727
It can just check the string inside the path
728
and redirect traffic accordingly.
729
Okay.
730
Now, there's a third type of load balancer
731
called traffic manager.
732
I don't think this is on the exam,
733
but this does traffic load balancing
734
at the DNS level.
735
So that means when you enter example.com in your browser,
736
it makes the determination then where to send your traffic.
737
If you are in Asia,
738
you can have the traffic being sent to a server in Asia.
739
If you're in America,
740
it can send the traffic to America and Europe.
741
And that's all done at the DNS level
742
and that's called traffic manager.
743
Now, when you're setting up load balancers,
744
one of the things that you need to think about
745
is what happens when a server fails.
746
So let's say you have three web servers
747
serving web traffic identically.
748
Load balancer in front of it.
749
And then at some point,
750
one of those web servers stops working.
751
It just doesn't reply, it's stuck in a loop,
752
it's crashed, blue screen of death.
753
The load balancer is smart enough to know this
754
and this is called a health probe.
755
So what you're gonna want to do
756
is have the load balancer check with the server
757
every 30 seconds, something like this.
758
And if the server returns a good result, it keeps going.
759
And if the server does not return a good result,
760
it should stop sending traffic to that server.
761
Now, there's ways of doing more sophisticated like checks
762
where the server itself can then restart
763
or get redeployed and stuff like that.
764
The most basic thing to do is
765
to remove traffic from load balancing
766
if that server is not responding well.
767
And that's called health probe.
768
Anyways, that is the most basic layer
769
of what load balancing is,
770
application gateway versus load balancer
771
versus traffic manager.
772
And we basically come to the end
773
of the networking stuff within this exam.
774
Again, this was intended to be a really quick overview,
775
go back and watch the course
776
for any of these things in more detail.
777
But yeah, that's the requirements of AZ-104 for networking
778
and if you understand all that,
779
then you should be fine on the exam.

--- Renewing Your Administrator Certification ---

-: So in this video we're gonna talk about renewing
your Microsoft Azure Administrator Associate Certification.
Now up to six months in advance
from when your certification expires,
you're going to receive an email from Microsoft
with a subject Action Required,
and it will tell you that you have a certification
that's going to expire on a certain date.
And so again, you have six months to prepare for this.
Now, the renewal of the exam is completely different
than the initial taking of the exam.
The renewal is free
and it's done completely online from home.
There's no proctor, you're not registering
with your license, there's no webcam involved.
You're just taking an online multiple choice
question assessment, and if you pass,
you will renew your certification for another year.
So the initial expiration is one year
from the time that you took it,
and you need to do the online assessment
every year after that.
Another thing that is important to know
is that the renewal is not as comprehensive
as your initial test.
See if we go down here and we click
on Renew Your Certification,
it's gonna take us to this page here.
Now, I haven't signed into the Microsoft Learn site
so this is not personalized to me at all right now,
but this is the portal for the renewal
of the administrator certification.
Now you can see here that these are the topics
of the renewal.
At the time of recording,
it's talking about these eight topics.
Now in the original AZ104 exam
with the Azure Associate Certification,
there are dozens and dozens of topics.
So this assessment is only limited to these eight topics.
Now these topics are gonna be
things that are relatively new in the last 12 months
that are important for the exam.
For instance, it's talking about Dockers,
talking about Azure D and Nest,
in this case of administrator service endpoints,
Azure Backup, Kubernetes, and Azure AD.
These topics are what is focused for the renewal.
Now, the renewal is free,
and what you can do is if you don't pass the first time,

and after your second attempt,
you need to wait 24 hours between retakes.
